In your actual server/main.py, register this router on the existing app after it is created:
from server.proactive.router import router as proactive_routerapp.include_router(proactive_router)



Do not make another FastAPI() application just for this feature. Also, do not merge the bootstrap authentication above without replacing or adapting it to Aariya’s actual account, session and device authorization.
File 5 — server/proactive/source_client.py
Call emit_event() from a real monitor or task runner after it has observed and validated an event. Do not generate a new source event ID on a retry.
from __future__ import annotationsimport jsonimport osimport socketfrom dataclasses import dataclassfrom typing import Any, Mappingfrom urllib.error import HTTPError, URLErrorfrom urllib.parse import urlsplitfrom urllib.request import Request, urlopenfrom pydantic import ValidationErrorfrom .schemas import EventInclass ProactiveSourceError(RuntimeError):    def __init__(        self,        message: str,        *,        status_code: int | None = None,    ) -> None:        super().__init__(message)        self.status_code = status_code@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class SourceClientSettings:    base_url: str    source_token: str    timeout_seconds: float    @classmethod    def from_environment(cls) -> "SourceClientSettings":        base_url = os.environ.get(



Connect a real monitor to the source client
The source's actual event handler should call the client with facts it really observed. The following is a wiring pattern, not a runnable fake event:
from server.proactive.source_client import emit_eventdef report_observed_task_failure(    *,    task_run_id: str,    task_name: str,    actual_error_summary: str,    actual_occurred_at,    actual_evidence_references: list[str],) -> dict:    """    Call only from the actual failure handler.    All arguments must come from the real task runner.    Do not call this function merely to test notifications.    """    return emit_event({        # Stable identifier from the task system; reused on retry.        "source_event_id": task_run_id,        "event_type": "task_failure",        # Severity must be supplied by the monitor's real policy,        # not guessed from the error string.        "severity": get_configured_severity_for_task(task_name),        "title": f"Task failed: {task_name}",        "summary": actual_error_summary,        "occurred_at": actual_occurred_at,        "correlation_key": task_run_id,        "evidence_refs": actual_evidence_references,        "attributes": {            "task_name": task_name,            "task_run_id": task_run_id,        },    })



get_configured_severity_for_task() in that example is an integration point you must connect to an actual stored/configured policy. It is intentionally not defined with a fabricated default. Also ensure the monitor obtains IDs and evidence references from real records and that the source is authorized to emit task_failure.
File 6 — server/proactive/outbox.py
The queue persists a notification before an attempt begins. A job can be pending, in flight, delivered, dead-lettered, or cancelled. Each delivery attempt has its own record. The queue does not decide whom to contact or what to say; that data must come from real incident state and the real escalation policy.
from __future__ import annotationsimport jsonimport sqlite3import uuidfrom contextlib import closingfrom dataclasses import dataclassfrom datetime import datetime, timedelta, timezonefrom pathlib import Pathfrom typing import Any, Iterableclass OutboxConflict(RuntimeError):    passclass OutboxNotFound(LookupError):    passclass OutboxValidationError(ValueError):    pass@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class NotificationJob:    job_id: str    dedupe_key: str    incident_id: str | None    channel: str    recipient_id: str    payload: dict[str, Any]    state: str    attempts: int    max_attempts: int    available_at: str



File 7 — server/proactive/dispatcher.py
This dispatcher is intentionally provider-independent. A real push or VoIP adapter must implement the contract. If the provider is not configured, the worker should not claim a job for that channel.
from __future__ import annotationsimport osfrom dataclasses import dataclassfrom typing import Callable, Mapping, Protocolfrom .outbox import (    ClaimedNotification,    NotificationJob,    NotificationOutbox,)@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class DeliveryReceipt:    """Receipt actually returned by a real provider."""    provider_message_id: str | Noneclass NotificationProvider(Protocol):    def deliver(        self,        job: NotificationJob,        *,        idempotency_key: str,    ) -> DeliveryReceipt:        ...class DispatchConfigurationError(RuntimeError):    passclass ProviderDeliveryError(RuntimeError):    """Safe, structured provider failure."""



Critical distinction: delivered here means the configured provider returned a receipt and that receipt was saved. It does not necessarily mean the phone displayed the message or you read it. The client acknowledgement must be another authenticated event.
4. Missing module: importance assessment and escalation policy
The event foundation stores observations. It does not yet decide which incidents justify a message or call. This next module keeps importance assessment separate from authorization.
File 8 — server/proactive/importance.py
The policy must come from a validated configuration record in your real database or approved configuration service. This module deliberately has no default weights or threshold values.
from __future__ import annotationsfrom dataclasses import dataclassfrom enum import StrEnumfrom math import isfinitefrom typing import Mappingclass ContactChannel(StrEnum):    RECORD_ONLY = "record_only"    MESSAGE = "message"    PUSH = "push"    CALL = "call"class EvidenceQuality(StrEnum):    VERIFIED_TELEMETRY = "verified_telemetry"    AUTHENTICATED_REPORT = "authenticated_report"    INFERRED = "inferred"    UNVERIFIED = "unverified"@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class ImportanceInput:    incident_id: str    severity_score: float    urgency: float    confidence: float    impact: float    time_pressure: float    evidence_quality_score: float    reversibility_risk: float    response_deadline: str | None    source_ids: tuple[str, ...]



Important before deployment: the database-backed policy loader and its versioned schema still need to be connected. quiet_hours_channel must come from the user's approved settings and policy rules. A critical safety override must be explicit in those rules; it must not be implemented as a hidden constant here. The output is a recommendation to the escalation engine, not permission to perform an action.

5. Missing module: action authorization
Aariya's ability to phone you must not grant her permission to change your system. Every actual operation must pass a separate server-side authorization check immediately before execution.
File 9 — server/proactive/action_authorization.py
This is the authorization service contract. The repository implementations must read actual grants and approvals from Aariya's persistent identity/approval store.
from __future__ import annotationsimport hashlibimport jsonfrom dataclasses import dataclassfrom datetime import datetime, timezonefrom typing import Protocol@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class ActionProposal:    proposal_id: str    actor_id: str    action_name: str    resource_id: str    arguments: dict    risk_level: str    created_at: datetime@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class CapabilityGrant:    grant_id: str    actor_id: str    allowed_actions: frozenset[str]    allowed_resources: frozenset[str]    allowed_risk_levels: frozenset[str]    approval_required_risk_levels: frozenset[str]    expires_at: datetime    revoked_at: datetime | None@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class ApprovalRecord:    approval_id: str    proposal_id: str



The action executor must call authorize_action() immediately before executing the tool and record the grant/approval IDs in the audit record. A proposal cannot be changed after approval without invalidating the approval digest.

The GrantRepository and ApprovalRepository are deliberately interfaces, not fake stores. They must be backed by your real database and authenticated approval process. The next additional safeguards are per-tool argument validation, resource scoping, approval freshness, idempotency, execution audit, cancellation and post-execution verification.
6. Missing module: hybrid preference learning
Aariya should learn from actual user responses, but a single answer must not silently change her call policy. Keep these states distinct:
- Observed: a behavior or response was recorded.
- Learned: a tentative pattern has enough evidence under a configured learning policy.
- Confirmed: the user explicitly approved a preference.
File 10 — server/proactive/preference_learning.py
This is the service layer and repository contract. The repository must persist real observations and proposals. It must not be backed by a hardcoded list of user responses.
from __future__ import annotationsfrom dataclasses import dataclassfrom enum import StrEnumfrom typing import Protocolclass PreferenceStatus(StrEnum):    OBSERVED = "observed"    LEARNED = "learned"    CONFIRMED = "confirmed"@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class UserFeedback:    feedback_id: str    user_id: str    incident_id: str    preference_key: str    observed_value: str    response: str    outcome_reference: str    occurred_at: str@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class LearningPolicy:    policy_id: str    version: str    learnable_preference_keys: frozenset[str]    material_preference_keys: frozenset[str]    minimum_observations: int    minimum_confidence: float    def validate(self) -> None:        if not self.policy_id or not self.version:



Required remaining persistence work: implement PreferenceRepository over the real database, including independent-observation counting, versioned records, audit history and transaction-safe confirmation. The UI must use the existing authenticated session to confirm a proposal. Do not treat a call being declined once as permanent permission to suppress all future critical calls.

7. Missing module: independent Guardian/Watchdog
The Guardian must run in a separate process and preferably a separate failure domain. It cannot reliably report that the main Aariya server has failed by depending only on that same server to deliver the alert.

The Guardian needs real configured health endpoints and a separately provisioned dispatch channel. The following is the health-probe core; it is not yet a complete independently hosted disaster-recovery system.
File 11 — server/guardian/probes.py
from __future__ import annotationsimport jsonimport osimport timefrom dataclasses import dataclassfrom typing import Anyfrom urllib.error import HTTPError, URLErrorfrom urllib.parse import urlsplitfrom urllib.request import Request, urlopenclass GuardianConfigurationError(RuntimeError):    pass@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class ProbeTarget:    service_id: str    health_url: str    expected_statuses: frozenset[int]@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class ProbeResult:    service_id: str    healthy: bool    checked_at_unix: float    status_code: int | None    failure_code: str | Nonedef load_probe_targets() -> tuple[ProbeTarget, ...]:    raw = os.environ.get("AARIYA_GUARDIAN_PROBES_JSON", "").strip()    if not raw:



This module performs probes only. To meet the full Guardian requirement, add:
- Persistent service-health transitions and deduplication in a Guardian-owned database.
- A supervisor or service manager that restarts the Guardian independently of Aariya.
- A real configured alert adapter that can work when the main Aariya API is down.
- Alert receipts, retries and recovery notices.
- Health probes for the database, LLM/provider readiness, gateway, workers and delivery path—not just the HTTP server.
- A policy to avoid treating one transient timeout as definitive failure.
Do not place every health check in the same process as the subsystem it monitors. That would fail exactly when the system needs the monitoring.
8. Missing Flutter integration: real incident API and inbox
Your existing Flutter app should render incidents returned by the authenticated Aariya backend. It must not invent incidents locally, use a hardcoded user ID, or treat a local notification tap as proof that an incident is resolved.

The following is an additive client example. It uses package:http/http.dart; adapt it to the app's existing API client if it already uses another HTTP layer.
File 12 — lib/features/proactive/proactive_incident.dart
class ProactiveIncident {
  const ProactiveIncident({
    required this.id,
    required this.state,
    required this.severity,
    required this.title,
    required this.summary,
    required this.createdAt,
    required this.events,
    required this.evidenceReferences,
  });

  final String id;
  final String state;
  final String severity;
  final String title;
  final String summary;
  final DateTime createdAt;
  final List<Map<String, dynamic>> events;
  final List<String> evidenceReferences;

  factory ProactiveIncident.fromJson(Map<String, dynamic> json) {
    final rawEvents = json['events'];
    final rawReferences = <String>[];

    final events = rawEvents is List
        ? rawEvents.whereType<Map>().map(
            (item) => Map<String, dynamic>.from(item),
          ).toList()
        : <Map<String, dynamic>>[];

    for (final event in events) {
      final refs = event['evidence_refs'];
      if (refs is List) {
        for (final ref in refs) {
          if (ref is String && ref.trim().isNotEmpty) {
            rawReferences.add(ref);
          }
        }
      }
    }

    final id = json['incident_id'];
    final state = json['state'];
    final severity = json['severity'];
    final title = json['title'];
    final summary = json['summary'];
    final createdAt = DateTime.tryParse(
      json['created_at']?.toString() ?? '',
    );

    if (id is! String ||
        state is! String ||
        severity is! String ||
        title is! String ||
        summary is! String ||
        createdAt == null) {
      throw const FormatException(
        'The server returned an invalid incident record.',
      );
    }

    return ProactiveIncident(
      id: id,
      state: state,
      severity: severity,
      title: title,
      summary: summary,
      createdAt: createdAt.toUtc(),
      events: events,
      evidenceReferences: rawReferences.toSet().toList(),
    );
  }
}


File 13 — lib/features/proactive/proactive_api.dart
The API origin and access token are injected from the real app configuration and authentication provider. This client does not store credentials itself.
import 'dart:convert';

import 'package:http/http.dart' as http;

import 'proactive_incident.dart';

typedef AccessTokenProvider = Future<String> Function();

class ProactiveApiException implements Exception {
  const ProactiveApiException(this.message, {this.statusCode});

  final String message;
  final int? statusCode;

  @override
  String toString() => message;
}

class ProactiveApi {
  ProactiveApi({
    required Uri apiOrigin,
    required AccessTokenProvider getAccessToken,
    required http.Client client,
  })  : _apiOrigin = apiOrigin,
        _getAccessToken = getAccessToken,
        _client = client {
    if (apiOrigin.scheme != 'https' &&
        !const {'localhost', '127.0.0.1', '::1'}
            .contains(apiOrigin.host)) {
      throw ArgumentError(
        'HTTPS is required outside loopback development.',
      );
    }

    if (apiOrigin.host.isEmpty ||
        apiOrigin.userInfo.isNotEmpty ||
        apiOrigin.hasQuery ||
        apiOrigin.hasFragment) {
      throw ArgumentError('Invalid Aariya API origin.');
    }
  }

  final Uri _apiOrigin;
  final AccessTokenProvider _getAccessToken;
  final http.Client _client;

  Uri _endpoint(String path) => _apiOrigin.resolve(
        '/api/proactive/v1/$path',
      );

  Future<Map<String, String>> _headers() async {
    final token = await _getAccessToken();

    if (token.trim().isEmpty) {
      throw const ProactiveApiException(
        'The current session is not authenticated.',
      );
    }

    return {
      'Authorization': 'Bearer $token',
      'Accept': 'application/json',
      'Content-Type': 'application/json',
    };
  }

  Future<dynamic> _request(
    String method,
    Uri uri, {
    Object? body,
  }) async {
    final headers = await _headers();
    final http.Response response;

    try {
      switch (method) {
        case 'GET':
          response = await _client.get(uri, headers: headers);
          break;
        case 'POST':
          response = await _client.post(
            uri,
            headers: headers,
            body: body == null ? null : jsonEncode(body),
          );
          break;
        default:
          throw ArgumentError('Unsupported HTTP method.');
      }
    } on ProactiveApiException {
      rethrow;
    } catch (_) {
      // Do not expose raw network exceptions or headers in the UI.
      throw const ProactiveApiException(
        'Could not reach the Aariya service.',
      );
    }

    if (response.statusCode < 200 ||
        response.statusCode >= 300) {
      // The UI receives the status, not an unfiltered server response body.
      throw ProactiveApiException(
        'Aariya request failed.',
        statusCode: response.statusCode,
      );
    }

    if (response.body.isEmpty) return null;

    try {
      return jsonDecode(response.body);
    } on FormatException {
      throw const ProactiveApiException(
        'Aariya returned an invalid response.',
      );
    }
  }

  Future<List<ProactiveIncident>> listIncidents({
    String? state,
  }) async {
    final uri = _endpoint('incidents').replace(
      queryParameters: {
        if (state != null && state.isNotEmpty) 'state': state,
      },
    );

    final result = await _request('GET', uri);

    if (result is! List) {
      throw const ProactiveApiException(
        'Incident response has an invalid format.',
      );
    }

    return result
        .whereType<Map>()
        .map(
          (json) => ProactiveIncident.fromJson(
            Map<String, dynamic>.from(json),
          ),
        )
        .toList();
  }

  Future<ProactiveIncident> getIncident(String incidentId) async {
    final result = await _request(
      'GET',
      _endpoint('incidents/${Uri.encodeComponent(incidentId)}'),
    );

    if (result is! Map) {
      throw const ProactiveApiException(
        'Incident response has an invalid format.',
      );
    }

    return ProactiveIncident.fromJson(
      Map<String, dynamic>.from(result),
    );
  }

  Future<ProactiveIncident> acknowledge(String incidentId) async {
    final result = await _request(
      'POST',
      _endpoint(
        'incidents/${Uri.encodeComponent(incidentId)}/acknowledge',
      ),
    );

    if (result is! Map) {
      throw const ProactiveApiException(
        'Acknowledgement response has an invalid format.',
      );
    }

    return ProactiveIncident.fromJson(
      Map<String, dynamic>.from(result),
    );
  }

  Future<ProactiveIncident> resolve(
    String incidentId, {
    required String reason,
  }) async {
    if (reason.trim().isEmpty) {
      throw ArgumentError('A resolution reason is required.');
    }

    final result = await _request(
      'POST',
      _endpoint(
        'incidents/${Uri.encodeComponent(incidentId)}/resolve',
      ),
      body: {'reason': reason.trim()},
    );

    if (result is! Map) {
      throw const ProactiveApiException(
        'Resolution response has an invalid format.',
      );
    }

    return ProactiveIncident.fromJson(
      Map<String, dynamic>.from(result),
    );
  }

  void close() => _client.close();
}


The ProactiveApiException catch clause around _request() is unnecessary in most implementations because the code below it throws only ProactiveApiException for local validation and ArgumentError for unsupported methods. You may remove that catch without changing the network error handling. In your integration, make sure the API client shares the app's HTTP-client lifecycle rather than closing a client that other features own.
File 14 — lib/features/proactive/proactive_inbox.dart
This screen displays actual incidents retrieved from the server. Acknowledge is an API action; opening a screen is not treated as acknowledgement.
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

import 'proactive_api.dart';
import 'proactive_incident.dart';

class ProactiveInbox extends StatefulWidget {
  const ProactiveInbox({
    super.key,
    required this.api,
  });

  final ProactiveApi api;

  @override
  State<ProactiveInbox> createState() => _ProactiveInboxState();
}

class _ProactiveInboxState extends State<ProactiveInbox> {
  late Future<List<ProactiveIncident>> _incidents;

  @override
  void initState() {
    super.initState();
    _reload();
  }

  void _reload() {
    _incidents = widget.api.listIncidents();
  }

  Future<void> _acknowledge(String incidentId) async {
    try {
      await widget.api.acknowledge(incidentId);
      if (!mounted) return;
      setState(_reload);
    } on ProactiveApiException catch (error) {
      if (!mounted) return;
      ScaffoldMessenger.of(context).showSnackBar(
        SnackBar(content: Text(error.message)),
      );
    }
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(
        title: const Text('Aariya incidents'),
        actions: [
          IconButton(
            tooltip: 'Refresh incidents',
            onPressed: () => setState(_reload),
            icon: const Icon(Icons.refresh),
          ),
        ],
      ),
      body: FutureBuilder<List<ProactiveIncident>>(
        future: _incidents,
        builder: (context, snapshot) {
          if (snapshot.connectionState == ConnectionState.waiting) {
            return const Center(
              child: CircularProgressIndicator(),
            );
          }

          if (snapshot.hasError) {
            return Center(
              child: Padding(
                padding: const EdgeInsets.all(24),
                child: Column(
                  mainAxisSize: MainAxisSize.min,
                  children: [
                    const Icon(Icons.cloud_off, size: 40),
                    const SizedBox(height: 12),
                    const Text(
                      'Incidents could not be loaded.',
                      textAlign: TextAlign.center,
                    ),
                    const SizedBox(height: 12),
                    FilledButton(
                      onPressed: () => setState(_reload),
                      child: const Text('Try again'),
                    ),
                  ],
                ),
              ),
            );
          }

          final incidents = snapshot.data ?? const [];

          if (incidents.isEmpty) {
            return const Center(
              child: Text('No incidents were returned by Aariya.'),
            );
          }

          return RefreshIndicator(
            onRefresh: () async => setState(_reload),
            child: ListView.separated(
              physics: const AlwaysScrollableScrollPhysics(),
              itemCount: incidents.length,
              separatorBuilder: (_, __) => const Divider(height: 1),
              itemBuilder: (context, index) {
                final incident = incidents[index];

                return ListTile(
                  leading: const Icon(Icons.notifications_active_outlined),
                  title: Text(incident.title),
                  subtitle: Text(
                    '${incident.severity.toUpperCase()} · '
                    '${incident.state}\n'
                    '${incident.summary}',
                    maxLines: 4,
                    overflow: TextOverflow.ellipsis,
                  ),
                  isThreeLine: true,
                  onTap: () => showModalBottomSheet<void>(
                    context: context,
                    isScrollControlled: true,
                    builder: (context) => SafeArea(
                      child: SingleChildScrollView(
                        padding: const EdgeInsets.all(20),
                        child: Column(
                          crossAxisAlignment: CrossAxisAlignment.start,
                          mainAxisSize: MainAxisSize.min,
                          children: [
                            Text(
                              incident.title,
                              style: Theme.of(context)
                                  .textTheme
                                  .titleLarge,
                            ),
                            const SizedBox(height: 8),
                            Text(incident.summary),
                            const SizedBox(height: 12),
                            Text('Status: ${incident.state}'),
                            Text('Severity: ${incident.severity}'),
                            Text('ID: ${incident.id}'),
                            const SizedBox(height: 12),
                            const Text(
                              'Evidence references are shown only as '
                              'identifiers here. Open them through an '
                              'authorized evidence endpoint; do not fetch '
                              'arbitrary URLs from event data.',
                            ),
                            for (final reference
                                in incident.evidenceReferences)
                              SelectableText(reference),
                          ],
                        ),
                      ),
                    ),
                  ),
                  trailing: incident.state == 'open'
                      ? IconButton(
                          tooltip: 'Acknowledge incident',
                          icon: const Icon(Icons.done),
                          onPressed: () => _acknowledge(incident.id),
                        )
                      : null,
                );
              },
            ),
          );
        },
      ),
    );
  }
}

They must be integrated into the app, not simulated by adding a screen.

10. Voice calls, shared conversation and action execution
These requirements cannot be completed just by adding the notification queue. They need to connect to Aariya's existing Brain and the real call provider.

The integration must obey these rules:
1. A call request references a real incident, an authenticated user and a permitted target device.
2. The provider returns the real call ID and status; authenticated webhooks update the call state.
3. When answered, the call opens an incident context in the existing Aariya conversation runtime.
4. The current voice state, context, reasoning, voice direction and TTS system handle the conversation.
5. A user request such as “fix it” is clarified if ambiguous.
6. The action is checked by authorize_action() immediately before execution.
7. Only the existing allow-listed executor performs the operation; the result is verified against actual system state.
8. The incident and previous conversation are preserved. Resolving the incident doesn't erase the earlier conversation.
Provider contract
The provider-specific implementation must be selected and configured in your deployment. This contract is the boundary the real provider must implement; it is not a fake working phone service.
from dataclasses import dataclassfrom enum import StrEnumfrom typing import Protocolclass CallState(StrEnum):    REQUESTED = "requested"    RINGING = "ringing"    ANSWERED = "answered"    DECLINED = "declined"    NO_ANSWER = "no_answer"    ENDED = "ended"    FAILED = "failed"    CANCELLED = "cancelled"@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class CallRequest:    call_request_id: str    incident_id: str    authenticated_user_id: str    authorized_device_id: str    reason: str    expires_at: str    idempotency_key: str@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class ProviderCallReceipt:    provider_call_id: str    initial_state: CallState    provider_request_id: strclass InternetCallProvider(Protocol):    def start_call(



A call state must only change after a verified provider response/webhook or an authenticated internal transition. Webhook signatures are provider-specific, so I won't invent a generic signature verifier and call it production-ready.
Conversation bridge contract
Do not create another LLM instance just for incident calls. Connect the incident context to Aariya's existing conversation controller through an adapter:

This is an integration interface, not an implementation of the existing controller. Its methods must be mapped to your actual Aariya session/conversation implementation after reviewing those source files. The same applies to the voice director and TTS.
11. Missing module: multi-device routing without duplicate calls
Aariya needs a real device registry for the authenticated account. The router must not simply send every call to every device. Select one eligible voice-call target, then apply the user's stored fallback policy if it becomes unavailable.

12. Encryption, evidence privacy and gateway trust
These are requirements, but they should not be implemented by inventing a home-grown cryptographic protocol.

Aariya must decide and document the trust boundary:
- Device ↔ Aariya AI Core: authenticate both endpoints, use TLS in transit, and use application-level message encryption if the gateway is not trusted with plaintext.
- Communication gateway: route authenticated sessions and encrypted payloads; do not use it as an independent reasoning system. If it must inspect plaintext, explicitly include it in the trusted-processing boundary.
- Stored incidents and evidence: encrypt sensitive storage and backups using managed keys, restrict retrieval by the authenticated user and incident grant, and log access without copying secrets into logs.
- Evidence delivery: use opaque evidence identifiers and a server-side authorization check. Never fetch arbitrary URLs supplied in an event; don't place private logs or full evidence files in push notification payloads.
- Device security: store device credentials in the platform's secure storage, allow revocation, rotate keys under an explicit key lifecycle and re-authenticate sensitive actions.
- Calls and approval: a voice call or a voice phrase alone must not approve a sensitive operation. Use authenticated in-app confirmation or a stronger approval flow.
The actual encryption implementation depends on your chosen key-management and mobile/real-time transport stack. The necessary code should follow those provider/platform contracts rather than claiming encryption works just because a field named encrypted_payload exists.
13. Additional Aariya requirement: startup readiness before launching
You also described an adjacent requirement in your Aariya development work: the readiness sequence must run before starting the brain, Vite UI, or GUI. It must inspect the real capabilities and dependencies, including the LLM/provider status, database, required services, and application health; detect registered capabilities that are no longer available; and block startup when a required component fails.

For the package.json you previously shared, the launch commands must go through readiness-aware launchers instead of bypassing the check:

  }

This requires the corresponding startup engine and launcher modules to exist. Changing the script names alone does not implement the checks. The capability inventory must be based on registered capability contracts and actual health/readiness results—not guessed statuses or fabricated successful checks.

Next step: connect the decision engine to reliable delivery
The next step is now implemented as a new backend module: server/proactive/escalation_delivery.py.

This closes the gap between Aariya deciding that an incident deserves attention and safely saving a notification job for a real device. It does not choose importance thresholds or recipients itself; those must come from the configured policy and authenticated device registry.

The implementation has been tested against the existing event store and notification outbox: 32 tests pass, including six new tests for this bridge.
1. Create server/proactive/escalation_delivery.py
Paste this file into the existing proactive package.
"""Transactional bridge from a real policy decision to durable delivery jobs.Does not decide importance, choose recipients, or deliver notifications.Persists the decision and its outbox jobs in one SQLite transaction.Single-host SQLite implementation. Multi-instance deployments should use ashared transactional database/queue with equivalent guarantees."""from __future__ import annotationsimport hashlibimport jsonimport mathimport sqlite3import uuidfrom contextlib import closingfrom dataclasses import dataclassfrom datetime import datetime, timezonefrom pathlib import Pathfrom typing import Any, Mapping, Sequenceclass EscalationDeliveryError(RuntimeError):    """Base exception for escalation delivery errors."""class IncidentNotFound(EscalationDeliveryError):    passclass DecisionConflict(EscalationDeliveryError):    """A stable idempotency key was reused with different content."""class IntegrationNotInitialized(EscalationDeliveryError):    pass

These are initialization statements, not a replacement FastAPI app. Put them in Aariya's existing lifecycle/startup handling and ensure storage failures stop the feature from being reported as ready.
3. Connect it to the real policy decision
After the importance engine and escalation policy have evaluated a real persisted event, the integration should follow this pattern:
from server.proactive.escalation_delivery import (    EscalationDecision,    DeliveryIntent,    make_decision_key,)def persist_contact_decision(    *,    incident_id: str,    persisted_trigger_id: str,    user_id_from_authorized_registry: str,    policy_record_id: str,    policy_version: str,    score_from_assessment: float,    confidence_from_assessment: float,    chosen_channel: str,    rationale_from_policy: str,    requires_verification: bool,    authorized_delivery_intents: tuple[DeliveryIntent, ...],):    decision_key = make_decision_key(        incident_id=incident_id,        trigger_id=persisted_trigger_id,        policy_record_id=policy_record_id,        policy_version=policy_version,    )    decision = EscalationDecision(        decision_key=decision_key,        incident_id=incident_id,        user_id=user_id_from_authorized_registry,        policy_record_id=policy_record_id,        policy_version=policy_version,        trigger_id=persisted_trigger_id,        score=score_from_assessment,



Every input in this example must be resolved from real services. The user ID must come from the actual account/incident ownership mapping; the trigger ID must be a persisted event or revision ID; and the recipient IDs and retry settings must come from the real device registry and approved policy. Do not call this with manually invented operational values.
4. Important behavior
The bridge will now:
- Persist the escalation decision and notification jobs atomically.
- Return the existing decision on an identical retry.
- Reject a reused decision key if its content has changed.
- Suppress contact if the incident is already resolved.
- Refuse to enqueue contact when verification is required.
- Record a decision even when the correct outcome is to record only or wait for verification.
The dispatcher must still check the current authoritative incident state immediately before delivery. An incident may be resolved after a job was queued. Also, the source token and single configured user token in the earlier foundation are bootstrap authentication only; the live integration must use Aariya's actual account/session and per-device authorization.
5. Verification and next stage
I added tests/test_escalation_delivery.py to the existing test suite. The six new tests cover atomic decision/job persistence, retry idempotency, changed-content rejection, resolved-incident suppression, verification-required rejection and record-only decisions.

The combined foundation suite passes 32 tests, and Python compilation passes.

The next stage after this bridge is to make the importance policy and escalation settings persisted, versioned, admin/user-configurable records, then connect the real device registry. Only after that can a real push provider and VoIP service be wired into the dispatcher. The Flutter incident inbox and Guardian then need integration with the current Aariya source code.

1. Eight additions I recommend
P0

1. Per-user and per-device authorization
Must fix first
The current foundation uses a bootstrap user token and does not yet demonstrate complete incident ownership checks for a multi-user, multi-device deployment. Add explicit user/tenant ownership to incidents, sources, notifications, evidence, conversations and action grants. Verify authorization on every read, update, delivery and evidence request. Device revocation must take effect immediately.



P0

2. Versioned, persistent policy and user settings

Store escalation rules, importance thresholds, quiet hours, presence modes, fallback preferences, retry limits and allowed overrides in an approved, versioned configuration store. Record which policy version produced each decision. New policy versions need validation, approval where appropriate, rollback and an audit trail. Do not invent default thresholds when configuration is missing.



P0

3. A real push and VoIP integration

Register and revoke actual devices, securely store provider credentials, validate provider webhooks, and persist real delivery/call events. Distinguish a queued request from provider acceptance, device delivery, an answered call, user acknowledgement and a verified resolution. No provider success may be inferred.



P1

4. Alert storms, rate limits and cost controls

Correlate repeated failures, consolidate simultaneous incidents, apply per-user/source/channel limits and introduce circuit breakers for failing providers. Keep a critical incident visible even when notifications are rate-limited. Record why a notification was suppressed rather than silently dropping it.



P1

5. Event freshness, replay protection and recovery verification

Add source sequence numbers where supported, event expiry, timestamp validation, source credential rotation, replay handling and stale-event rules. Distinguish a user dismissing an alert from a monitor confirming that a problem has been fixed. Allow incidents to reopen if a new verified failure occurs.



P1

6. Tamper-evident audit and evidence integrity

Add an append-only, hash-linked audit trail with restricted write access and independent backup or anchoring. Record evidence provenance, access attempts, policy decisions, provider receipts, approvals and actual verification results. A checksum proves data consistency, not that a claim is truthful.



P1

7. Recovery and observability

Monitor queue lag, retry counts, dead-letter jobs, provider failures, database health and event-source heartbeat. Test backup restoration, worker crashes, duplicate callbacks, revoked devices, expired approvals and partial service outages. The Guardian needs its own failure domain and an independent way to report critical problems.



P2

8. Privacy and learning controls

Add user-visible monitoring controls, data retention and deletion rules, evidence export, consent for fallback channels, sensitive-notification preview settings and a way to review or undo learned preferences. Treat text in events, logs, web content and evidence as untrusted input—not instructions to the AI.



2. One more important addition: the capability registry
This connects directly to your existing Aariya startup-readiness requirement.

Aariya should maintain a real capability registry. Each integration should identify its owner, readiness requirements, health check, permissions, dependencies and version. The startup sequence should compare registered capabilities against actual runtime readiness rather than simply checking whether a file exists.

For example, the LLM capability should distinguish:
- configured: credentials and required configuration exist;
- ready: a real readiness probe succeeded;
- degraded: the service is available but a required function is failing;
- unavailable: a required dependency failed;
- unknown: readiness could not be verified.
A configured API key is not proof that an LLM is reachable. Likewise, a running HTTP server is not proof that Aariya’s brain, memory database, voice pipeline or notification delivery is operational.
3. The next implementation order
I recommend this order so the feature remains one integrated system:
Order	Implementation	Acceptance condition
1	User ownership and scoped authorization	No user or device can access another user's incidents or evidence
2	Persistent, versioned policy settings	Every escalation decision can be reproduced from its recorded policy version and inputs
3	Incident-to-outbox transaction and delivery lifecycle	No accepted decision silently loses its notification job
4	Registered device and real push provider	A real provider receipt and authenticated callback update delivery state
5	Real VoIP integration and shared Aariya session	Answering a call connects to the existing Aariya conversation runtime
6	Action authorization and verified execution	No tool acts without current permission; actual outcomes are verified
7	Independent Guardian and outage dispatch	Critical failure can be detected and reported when the main Core is down
8	Flutter integration, security tests and operational recovery	The end-to-end flow works against real services with no fabricated runtime events

My recommendation: do not add another standalone feature yet. Fix the ownership/authorization gap first, then persist the policy settings and connect the decision pipeline to delivery. Those are the foundations on which every other feature depends.

1. Seven additional requirements

1. Monitoring coverage and source heartbeats
Highest priority
Aariya must detect when a monitor stops reporting. Silence must not be interpreted as a healthy system. Track each source’s last successful observation, expected reporting interval, health, credential status and coverage gaps. An unavailable monitor should generate a separate, evidence-backed health incident when policy permits.




2. Safe action retries and recovery

Every executable action needs an idempotency key, execution record, timeout handling and result verification. If a request times out after a potentially destructive action, Aariya must check whether it happened before retrying. Support cancellation and compensation where genuinely possible, and record when rollback is unavailable.




3. Emergency controls and kill switches

Provide independent controls to disable proactive calls, disable a particular provider, suspend a compromised source, or pause autonomous actions without shutting down all monitoring. These controls must be authenticated, audited and reflected in readiness checks. A disabled contact channel should not silently discard the underlying incident.




4. Policy simulation and decision replay

Before activating a new importance or escalation policy, evaluate it against recorded, access-controlled historical events. Compare how it would change calls, messages, suppression and false-positive rates. Provide a dry-run mode that cannot send notifications or execute actions, and an audited rollback to the previous approved policy version.




5. Incident corrections and conflicting evidence

A later event may contradict an earlier observation. Aariya needs explicit states for false positives, corrected findings, reopened incidents and merged or separated incidents. Preserve original events and append corrections instead of silently rewriting history. Reassess any pending notification or action when the underlying facts change.




6. Reliability targets and degraded operation

Define measurable targets for detection delay, queue processing, delivery attempts, recovery time and data loss. Specify recovery-time and recovery-point objectives, backup restoration tests and queue capacity limits. If the LLM is unavailable, deterministic safety rules should still be able to preserve and route eligible incidents without pretending reasoning is available.




7. Aariya Monitoring & Control Center

Give you one place to review authorized monitoring sources, capabilities and health, incidents, evidence, delivery attempts, trusted devices, presence mode, call preferences, permission grants, learned preferences and policy changes. Include a clear history explaining why Aariya contacted you and what she actually did.

The event lifecycle should now include source-health detection, correction handling and action recovery:

3. What I would implement next
Don't add more large features until these foundations work together. I recommend this order:
Priority	Implementation	Completion condition
P0	Source heartbeats and coverage	A stopped or unhealthy monitor can be detected independently
P0	Action idempotency and outcome reconciliation	Timeouts cannot blindly trigger duplicate destructive actions
P0	Scoped authorization and emergency controls	Revoked permissions take effect, and contact/actions can be safely suspended
P1	Policy versions, simulation and replay	Policy changes can be evaluated and rolled back
P1	Incident corrections and recovery lifecycle	Contradictory evidence updates the incident without erasing history
P1	Reliability targets and failure testing	Restart, outage, backup and recovery behavior are demonstrably tested
P2	Monitoring & Control Center	You can inspect and control the real system from one interface

One important distinction about your no-fabricated-data requirement: production operation must use actual observations, real database records, real provider receipts and verified outcomes. Automated tests can use clearly isolated test fixtures, but test data must never enter the operational event stream or be presented as real incidents.

1. Six final requirements to add

1. Security threat modelling and adversarial testing

Test forged monitoring events, replayed webhooks, compromised device credentials, prompt injection through logs or evidence, unauthorized resource access, secret leakage and malicious provider responses. Every integration must have explicit trust boundaries and least-privilege permissions.
Critical




2. Complete data lifecycle and key destruction

Define retention periods for incident records, evidence, call transcripts, audio, device metadata and audit records. Support authorized export, deletion, legal holds, backup expiry, key rotation and secure disposal. Deleting a user account must not leave ungoverned copies behind.




3. Voice privacy and recording controls

Establish whether calls are recorded or transcribed, when consent is required, who can access the content, how long it is retained and how recording can be disabled. Avoid collecting audio merely because a call capability exists. Apply the relevant laws where the user and other participants are located.




4. Time, locale and accessibility correctness

Handle time zones, daylight-saving transitions, clock drift, deadlines, language preferences, voice accessibility and notification presentation. A deadline must use a clear time zone; a user's “sleeping” window must not be interpreted using an arbitrary server timezone.




5. Failure communication and emergency boundaries

Aariya must explain when she cannot reach you, when a provider is unavailable, and when an incident remains unverified. Never imply that a message was read, a call was answered or an emergency service was contacted without proof. Configure any external emergency-contact or fallback route explicitly and obtain the necessary consent.




6. Production release gates and recovery runbooks

Require automated tests, migration checks, security review, restore tests, rollback instructions and explicit readiness checks before release. Maintain an operator runbook for a failed database, stuck notification queue, compromised source token, failed voice provider and unavailable AI Core.



2. Add these acceptance criteria to the specification
Aariya should not be considered production-ready until these conditions are met:
- [ ] A user cannot access another user's incidents, recordings, evidence or device sessions.
- [ ] A compromised or revoked credential can no longer authorize new operations.
- [ ] A monitoring outage is detectable without relying exclusively on the monitored service.
- [ ] A failed or timed-out action is reconciled before any potentially duplicate execution.
- [ ] A policy change is versioned, audited, tested and reversible.
- [ ] A provider outage does not silently erase queued incidents or falsely report delivery.
- [ ] Data deletion, backup restoration and key rotation have been tested.
- [ ] Every release has verified security, recovery and integration test results.
Test fixtures are acceptable in isolated automated tests, but operational incidents, recipients, credentials, policy decisions and provider results must all be real and traceable. 
3. My recommendation: stop expanding the architecture
Freeze the requirements. Start integration.
The next milestone should be an end-to-end vertical slice—not another standalone module:

Real monitor → persisted event → versioned policy decision → authorized device → real notification → user response → existing Aariya conversation → permission check → verified outcome → audited incident record.

Then test what happens when each component fails, restarts, times out or returns contradictory information.

Aariya next milestone: requirements-to-code matrix and integration tests
The next milestone is to prove that Aariya's real event pipeline works end to end, then integrate it into the existing backend and Flutter app.

I added one integration test to the available source overlay and ran the suite again: 33 tests passed. This test covers the path from authenticated event ingestion through persistent incident storage, escalation decision persistence and outbox enqueueing. It does not send a real push notification or initiate a call.

3. End-to-end integration test plan
These are the next tests I would require before enabling proactive contact.
Test	Scenario	Required result
E2E-01	Authorized monitor reports an event	Event and incident are persisted
E2E-02	Same event is submitted again	Existing event is returned; no duplicate incident
E2E-03	Existing event ID is reused with changed content	Request is rejected
E2E-04	Monitor reports an unauthorized event type	Request is rejected
E2E-05	Identical policy decision is retried	Same decision/job is returned
E2E-06	Incident is already resolved before enqueueing	No contact job is created
E2E-07	Incident resolves after enqueueing but before dispatch	Dispatcher rechecks authoritative state and cancels the job
E2E-08	Worker crashes after claiming a job	Expired lease permits recovery without uncontrolled duplication
E2E-09	Provider returns a transient error	Retry is scheduled according to the persisted policy
E2E-10	Provider returns a permanent rejection	Job reaches a terminal failure state and is observable
E2E-11	User/device permission is revoked	Further access and delivery are denied
E2E-12	One provider is down	Incident remains persisted; configured fallback is evaluated
E2E-13	LLM is unavailable	Event ingestion and deterministic safety policy remain available where designed
E2E-14	Flutter acknowledges an incident	Server records acknowledgement; incident is not automatically resolved
E2E-15	A call is answered	The existing Aariya conversation session receives the incident context
E2E-16	Voice request asks for an unauthorized action	No action is executed
E2E-17	Action request times out	Actual system state is reconciled before retry
E2E-18	Guardian detects a Core outage	The independent alert route is used without depending on the failed Core

Tests involving real provider receipts, external webhooks, iOS/Android call behavior or an independently deployed Guardian remain blocked until those real integrations exist. Their successful behavior must not be inferred from unit tests.
4. What to integrate next in the actual project
The next practical order is:
1. Connect the router to the existing FastAPI application's authentication and database/migration system.
2. Connect actual task and system monitors to event ingestion.
3. Load importance thresholds, user preferences and contact permissions from a persisted, versioned policy store.
4. Connect the decision pipeline to the durable outbox and a real push provider.
5. Integrate the Flutter incident inbox and authenticated evidence viewing.
6. Add VoIP calls through the existing Aariya conversation runtime.
7. Finish action authorization, the independent Guardian, key management and recovery testing.

1. Does the implementation plan follow the requirements?
Phase	Does it follow the plan?	What must be true before proceeding
1. Integrate authentication and database	Yes — start here	Use Aariya's existing identity/session system, enforce incident ownership, and integrate migrations.
2. Connect real event sources	Yes	Actual monitors report authenticated observations with stable IDs and genuine evidence.
3. Add persisted importance and escalation policy	Yes	Policies and preferences come from approved, versioned records, not invented defaults.
4. Connect decisions to the outbox	Yes	The decision and delivery jobs are persisted atomically, and duplicate decisions are handled safely.
5. Connect real push notifications	Yes	Use actual credentials, authenticated provider callbacks and recorded delivery outcomes.
6. Integrate the Flutter app	Yes	Use real sessions, device registration, incident APIs and authorized evidence access.
7. Add internet voice calls	Yes	Answering a call connects to the existing Aariya conversation and voice pipeline.
8. Add action authorization	Yes	Each action is checked immediately before execution and its actual outcome is verified.
9. Deploy the independent Guardian	Yes	It can detect and report Core failures without depending entirely on the failed Core.
10. Run security and recovery tests	Yes	Actual integrations, failure recovery, authorization boundaries and deployment readiness are tested.

2. Four things I would fix before continuing
A. Enforce ownership on every endpoint

The current bootstrap token approach is not enough for production multi-device access. Every incident, event, evidence reference, device and action must be checked against the authenticated user's permissions. An authenticated user must never gain access to another user's incident simply by knowing its ID.



B. Make the policy authoritative

The escalation-delivery bridge validates the structure of a decision, but it does not itself prove that the decision came from the approved policy service or that its recipient is authorized. The policy service and device registry must establish those facts before a job is enqueued.



C. Keep the event, decision and delivery lifecycle consistent

Ensure incident changes invalidate stale decisions and pending actions. Recheck current incident state before dispatch and before executing an action. Acknowledgement must not imply resolution, and provider acceptance must not imply that you received or read a message.



D. Test actual integrations, not just modules

The uploaded document reports 33 passing tests, but those tests do not prove that a real push provider, VoIP call, Flutter session, Guardian deployment or complete authorization system works. Each needs its own integration tests and observable outcomes. Pasted markdown.md

I recommend this dependency order:

Next: audit the real Aariya codebase
I checked the Aariya package.json and launcher information available from your earlier work. Your current commands include npm run dev → scripts/dev.mjs, npm run dev:brain → scripts/dev-brain.mjs, npm run dev:ui → Vite, and npm run gui → Electron. 

Before merging the proactive modules, we need to find the real backend entry point, existing authentication and database, conversation/voice pipeline, Flutter client and any current notification integrations.

Instead of guessing, add this read-only source audit script. It scans the repository and reports relevant file paths and line numbers. It does not change application code, contact external services or read .env secrets.
1. Create scripts/audit-proactive-integration.mjs
/**
 * Aariya Proactive Integration Audit
 *
 * Run from the root of the actual Aariya repository:
 *   node scripts/audit-proactive-integration.mjs
 *
 * Read-only:
 * - Does not change source files.
 * - Does not read .env, credential or secret files.
 * - Does not call external services or the LLM.
 * - Reports paths and line numbers, not matching source contents.
 *
 * This is an inventory tool, not a readiness or security certification.
 */

import fs from "node:fs";
import path from "node:path";
import process from "node:process";

const ROOT = process.cwd();

const EXCLUDED_DIRS = new Set([
  ".git",
  "node_modules",
  ".venv",
  "venv",
  "__pycache__",
  ".dart_tool",
  "build",
  "dist",
  "coverage",
  ".next",
  ".gradle",
  "Pods",
  ".idea",
  ".aariya",
]);

const ALLOWED_EXTENSIONS = new Set([
  ".py",
  ".mjs",
  ".cjs",
  ".js",
  ".ts",
  ".tsx",
  ".jsx",
  ".dart",
  ".json",
  ".yaml",
  ".yml",
  ".toml",
]);

const MAX_FILE_BYTES = 1_000_000;
const MAX_FILES = 20_000;
const MAX_FINDINGS = 250;

function isSecretLikeFilename(name) {
  const lower = name.toLowerCase();

  return (
    lower === ".env" ||
    lower.startsWith(".env.") ||
    lower.includes("secret") ||
    lower.includes("credential") ||
    lower.endsWith(".pem") ||
    lower.endsWith(".key") ||
    lower.endsWith(".p12") ||
    lower.endsWith(".pfx")
  );
}

function relative(filePath) {
  return path.relative(ROOT, filePath).split(path.sep).join("/");
}

function exists(relativePath) {
  return fs.existsSync(path.join(ROOT, relativePath));
}

function walk(directory, results) {
  if (results.length >= MAX_FILES) return;

  let entries;

  try {
    entries = fs.readdirSync(directory, {
      withFileTypes: true,
    });
  } catch {
    return;
  }

  for (const entry of entries) {
    if (results.length >= MAX_FILES) return;

    const fullPath = path.join(directory, entry.name);

    if (entry.isDirectory()) {
      if (EXCLUDED_DIRS.has(entry.name)) continue;
      if (isSecretLikeFilename(entry.name)) continue;

      walk(fullPath, results);
      continue;
    }

    if (!entry.isFile()) continue;
    if (isSecretLikeFilename(entry.name)) continue;

    const extension = path.extname(entry.name).toLowerCase();

    if (!ALLOWED_EXTENSIONS.has(extension)) continue;

    try {
      const stat = fs.statSync(fullPath);

      if (stat.size > MAX_FILE_BYTES) continue;

      results.push(fullPath);
    } catch {
      // A file that disappears during an audit is omitted.
    }
  }
}

const SIGNALS = [
  {
    name: "fastapi_app",
    regex: /\bFastAPI\s*\(/,
  },
  {
    name: "api_router",
    regex: /\bAPIRouter\s*\(|include_router\s*\(/,
  },
  {
    name: "authentication",
    regex:
      /OAuth2PasswordBearer|HTTPBearer|current_user|AuthMiddleware|FirebaseAuth|verify_token|JWT_SECRET|jwt\.decode/i,
  },
  {
    name: "database",
    regex:
      /create_async_engine|create_engine|AsyncSession|SessionLocal|sqlite3|asyncpg|DATABASE_URL|MongoClient/i,
  },
  {
    name: "redis_or_queue",
    regex:
      /\bRedis\b|redis\.from_url|Celery\s*\(|dramatiq|arq\.create_pool|task_queue/i,
  },
  {
    name: "llm",
    regex:
      /\bOpenAI\s*\(|AsyncOpenAI|anthropic|model_provider|LLMClient|llm_client/i,
  },
  {
    name: "conversation",
    regex:
      /conversation_context|conversation_id|ConversationRuntime|conversation_history|ConversationManager/i,
  },
  {
    name: "voice",
    regex:
      /voice_director|voice_engine|SpeechToText|TextToSpeech|WebRTC|LiveKit|Agora|CallKit|flutter_callkit|tts_engine/i,
  },
  {
    name: "push_notifications",
    regex:
      /firebase_messaging|FirebaseMessaging|flutter_local_notifications|APNs|FCM|notification_service/i,
  },
  {
    name: "device_registry",
    regex:
      /device_registry|device_token|push_token|registered_device|trusted_device|device_revoked/i,
  },
  {
    name: "action_authorization",
    regex:
      /capability_grant|authorize_action|action_executor|tool_executor|permission_manager|approval_required/i,
  },
  {
    name: "health_and_readiness",
    regex:
      /health\/ready|readiness|health_check|healthcheck|check_real_required_dependencies/i,
  },
  {
    name: "guardian_and_monitoring",
    regex:
      /watchdog|guardian|heartbeat|health_monitor|source_heartbeat|monitoring_source/i,
  },
  {
    name: "startup_launchers",
    regex:
      /startup:check|startup-integrity|preflight|dev-brain\.mjs|strictPort|spawn\s*\(/i,
  },
  {
    name: "proactive_events",
    regex:
      /proactive_incidents|proactive_notification_outbox|EscalationDecision|NotificationOutbox|source_event_id/i,
  },
];

function scanSource(filePath) {
  let source;

  try {
    source = fs.readFileSync(filePath, "utf8");
  } catch {
    return null;
  }

  const lines = source.split(/\r?\n/);
  const matches = {};

  for (const signal of SIGNALS) {
    const lineNumbers = [];

    for (let index = 0; index < lines.length; index += 1) {
      if (signal.regex.test(lines[index])) {
        lineNumbers.push(index + 1);

        if (lineNumbers.length >= 30) break;
      }
    }

    if (lineNumbers.length > 0) {
      matches[signal.name] = lineNumbers;
    }
  }

  if (Object.keys(matches).length === 0) return null;

  return {
    path: relative(filePath),
    signals: matches,
  };
}

function getRootPackageScripts() {
  const packagePath = path.join(ROOT, "package.json");

  if (!fs.existsSync(packagePath)) return null;

  try {
    const pkg = JSON.parse(
      fs.readFileSync(packagePath, "utf8"),
    );

    // Scripts are command declarations, not environment secrets.
    return {
      name: pkg.name ?? null,
      scripts: pkg.scripts ?? {},
    };
  } catch {
    return {
      error: "Root package.json could not be parsed",
    };
  }
}

function findKnownEntrypoints(files) {
  const paths = new Set(files.map(relative));

  const candidates = [
    "package.json",
    "main.js",
    "server/main.py",
    "server/__init__.py",
    "server/agent/controller.py",
    "server/redis_manager.py",
    "server/llm.py",
    "server/retriever.py",
    "server/confidence.py",
    "server/proactive/router.py",
    "server/proactive/store.py",
    "server/proactive/outbox.py",
    "backend/app/main.py",
    "backend/app/api/router.py",
    "app/main.py",
    "pyproject.toml",
    "requirements.txt",
    "requirements-dev.txt",
    "pubspec.yaml",
    "lib/main.dart",
    "lib/app.dart",
    "scripts/dev.mjs",
    "scripts/dev-brain.mjs",
    "scripts/dev-ui.mjs",
    "scripts/gui.mjs",
    "scripts/backend.sh",
    "scripts/startup/engine.mjs",
    "scripts/audit-proactive-integration.mjs",
    "capabilities",
  ];

  return candidates.map((candidate) => ({
    path: candidate,
    exists: exists(candidate) || paths.has(candidate),
  }));
}

function summarizeSignals(findings) {
  const counts = Object.fromEntries(
    SIGNALS.map((signal) => [signal.name, 0]),
  );

  for (const finding of findings) {
    for (const name of Object.keys(finding.signals)) {
      counts[name] += 1;
    }
  }

  return counts;
}

function main() {
  if (!exists("package.json")) {
    console.error(
      "Run this script from the Aariya repository root, " +
      "where package.json is present.",
    );
    process.exitCode = 2;
    return;
  }

  const files = [];
  walk(ROOT, files);

  const findings = [];

  for (const file of files) {
    const result = scanSource(file);

    if (result) {
      findings.push(result);
    }
  }

  findings.sort((a, b) => a.path.localeCompare(b.path));

  const report = {
    audit: "Aariya Proactive Integration Inventory",
    generatedAt: new Date().toISOString(),
    repositoryRoot: ROOT,
    readOnly: true,
    secretsRead: false,
    filesScanned: files.length,
    fileLimitReached: files.length >= MAX_FILES,
    entrypoints: findKnownEntrypoints(files),
    package: getRootPackageScripts(),
    signalCounts: summarizeSignals(findings),
    findings: findings.slice(0, MAX_FINDINGS),
    findingsTruncated: findings.length > MAX_FINDINGS,
    limitations: [
      "A matching source pattern does not prove a feature works.",
      "No network/provider health checks were performed.",
      "No environment variables or secret files were read.",
      "This is an inventory, not a security certification.",
    ],
  };

  process.stdout.write(
    JSON.stringify(report, null, 2) + "\n",
  );
}

main();


2. Run it from your Aariya project root
First save the file above as scripts/audit-proactive-integration.mjs. Then run:
node scripts/audit-proactive-integration.mjs


To save the report to a file:

macOS/Linux
node scripts/audit-proactive-integration.mjs \
  > proactive-integration-audit.json


PowerShell
node scripts/audit-proactive-integration.mjs |
  Out-File -Encoding utf8 proactive-integration-audit.json


The report contains paths, line numbers, project scripts and signal categories—not source-code excerpts or .env contents. Review the report before sharing it, because repository paths can still reveal project structure.
3. What I will use the report for
The next implementation should be mapped against the actual code in this order:
1. Existing application and identity: find the real FastAPI app and current authentication/session dependencies.
2. Existing database: identify its connection/session lifecycle and migration system before adding proactive tables.
3. Existing Brain: locate the conversation controller, memory, voice director and permitted action executor.
4. Existing Flutter application: identify the app entry point, navigation, API client, session management and notification dependencies.
5. Existing launch readiness: determine whether the startup-integrity overlay is installed and whether every launcher follows it.
That mapping prevents duplicate services and helps preserve your existing Aariya implementation.

Next implementation: Aariya source heartbeats and monitoring coverage
I implemented the next P0 feature against the available proactive backend overlay and reran the tests. The suite now has 38 passing tests, up from 33.

This addresses one of the most important failure modes we identified: Aariya must not assume a monitored system is healthy simply because it has stopped reporting.

The code below is provided directly in chat. It adds heartbeat tracking, replay protection and health-status endpoints. It is not yet merged into your live Aariya repository, because its actual backend and Flutter source tree are still unavailable here.
1. Add server/proactive/source_health.py
This module stores the latest accepted heartbeat for each configured monitoring source. A source's expected reporting interval comes from configuration; there is no hardcoded operational interval.
"""Durable source heartbeats and monitoring coverage."""from __future__ import annotationsimport hashlibimport jsonimport sqlite3from contextlib import closingfrom datetime import datetime, timezonefrom pathlib import Pathfrom typing import Mappingfrom pydantic import (    BaseModel,    ConfigDict,    Field,    field_validator,)from .config import SourceAuthorizationclass HeartbeatConflict(RuntimeError):    """A heartbeat sequence was reused with different content."""class HeartbeatReplay(RuntimeError):    """A heartbeat is older than the latest accepted sequence."""class SourceHeartbeatIn(BaseModel):    model_config = ConfigDict(        extra="forbid",        str_strip_whitespace=True,    )



What this module does
- Records the server time at which a heartbeat was received.
- Rejects stale or conflicting sequences.
- Makes an identical retry idempotent without refreshing the liveness timestamp.
- Reports no_heartbeat when no heartbeat has ever arrived.
- Reports stale when the configured interval has elapsed.
- Reports unconfigured rather than assuming health when no interval exists.
- Uses a persistent store rather than memory-only status.
This stores the latest heartbeat checkpoint, not the complete heartbeat history. A production implementation should also add monitored health-state transitions to the audit trail and define retention for historical heartbeat records.

2. Update server/proactive/config.py
Add a heartbeat interval to each source authorization.

Modify the existing dataclass:
@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class SourceAuthorization:    token: str    allowed_event_types: frozenset[EventType]    heartbeat_interval_seconds: int | None = None



In Settings.from_environment(), allow the optional field in each source configuration. Replace the current source-entry key check with:
allowed_keys = {"token", "event_types"}allowed_keys_with_heartbeat = {    *allowed_keys,    "heartbeat_interval_seconds",}if not isinstance(entry, dict) or set(entry) not in (    allowed_keys,    allowed_keys_with_heartbeat,):    raise ConfigurationError(        "Each source must define token and event_types, "        "with optional heartbeat_interval_seconds"    )



After parsing and validating event_types, add:
interval = entry.get("heartbeat_interval_seconds")if interval is not None and (    isinstance(interval, bool)    or not isinstance(interval, int)    or not 5 <= interval <= 2_592_000):    raise ConfigurationError(        "heartbeat_interval_seconds must be an integer "        "from 5 to 2592000"    )validated_sources[source_id] = SourceAuthorization(    token=token,    allowed_event_types=event_types,    heartbeat_interval_seconds=interval,)



These limits are validation bounds, not the operational interval. Each actual source must have its interval deliberately configured.

Example configuration structure, using placeholders rather than credentials:
{
  "YOUR_REAL_MONITOR_ID": {
    "token": "<GENERATED_SOURCE_SECRET>",
    "event_types": ["task_failure"],
    "heartbeat_interval_seconds": 30
  }
}


Do not paste the placeholder into production. A monitor without an interval may send heartbeats, but its health remains unconfigured until the interval is set.

3. Add the endpoints to server/proactive/router.py
Add these imports:
from .source_health import (    HeartbeatConflict,    HeartbeatReplay,    HeartbeatReceipt,    SourceHealthOut,    SourceHealthStore,    SourceHeartbeatIn,)



Add a cached store factory and helper near the existing _store_for_path() function:
@lru_cache(maxsize=8)def _source_health_store_for_path(    path: str,) -> SourceHealthStore:    from pathlib import Path    return SourceHealthStore(Path(path))def _source_health_store(    settings: Settings,) -> SourceHealthStore:    try:        return _source_health_store_for_path(            str(settings.database_path)        )    except (OSError, RuntimeError, ValueError, sqlite3.Error) as exc:        raise HTTPException(            status_code=status.HTTP_503_SERVICE_UNAVAILABLE,            detail="Source-health storage is unavailable",        ) from exc

5. What this does not do yet
This step provides the source-health data and API. It does not yet:
- Run a background scheduler to evaluate sources continuously.
- Create a persisted incident when a source becomes stale.
- Send a notification that the monitor has stopped reporting.
- Detect a source that continues sending heartbeats while its actual subsystem is broken.
- Verify that a heartbeat accurately represents service health.
- Survive loss of the same database hosting the heartbeat record.
Those require the next module: a Source Health Evaluator and Guardian integration. It should evaluate configured intervals continuously, persist health-state transitions, open or update real monitoring incidents, and pass eligible alerts through the existing importance policy and durable outbox.

Next implementation: Source Health Evaluator + Guardian processing queue
This step adds two source files to the existing proactive backend overlay. It detects changes in monitoring-source health, persists those changes and creates a durable queue for a policy handler to process.

This validates the available source overlay, not a live integration with your Aariya deployment.
